PREFACE 

A new textbook in trigonometry must justify itself. Accepting this premise, the authors of this Plane Trigonometry have endeavored to bridge the gap between educational theory and practice. The oft-professed objectives of the teaching of mathematics have been given a central place in this book. An earnest attempt has been made to tie up the study of trigonometry with the previous courses in mathematics by using the graph and the function concept as unifying factors. 

Since mathematics is taught for its utilitarian values as well as for its merits as a logical system of thought, the authors have attempted to strike a proper balance between the practical applications and the analytical aspects of the subject. 

The arrangement is such that the book may be used on either the secondary school or college level. The teacher may, at his discretion, omit certain divisions of the book, if time does not permit a full course. Such sections of subject matter as 

De Moivre's Theorem and Polar Coordinates may be disregarded in a brief course.

The authors solicit the cooperation of teachers in preventing students from becoming enmeshed in meaningless symbolism. 

The arrangement of the book serves as a partial barrier against this tendency, but only the teacher himself can successfully deflect students from such a practice. 

The subject matter has been presented in simple English so that the average pupil will have no difficulty in understanding either the explanatory material or the directions for solving exercises. The diction used throughout the book is the simple language of the pupil so that the usual reading difficulty is eliminated. 

In the arrangement of illustrative examples and tabular forms an effort has been made to prevent undue eye-strain for the student.

The abundant illustrative material and the accurately drawn diagrams and graphs will help to make the subject of trigonometry vital and more easily comprehended.

vi 

PREFACE 

Fundamental concepts are frequently interrelated throughout the book so as to make fixation of principles permanent. 

Exercises have been carefully graded, ranging from the simplest to the most difficult. Sections of honor problems have been added to tax the minds of the better pupils. The frequent distribution of exercises to clinch important concepts immediately after they are developed, is an important feature. Experience has shown that the old-fashioned method of bunching all the exercises at the end of each chapter is inadequate for modern and effective teaching.

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY CHAPTER I TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

The study of trigonometry is simple and interesting. Do not allow either the sound or the size of the word to frighten you. From geometry you are familiar with the word polygon.

A trigon is simply a three-sided polygon, a triangle, and metry means measure. Hence the name Trigonometry. rise- run span ?

Recently a builder engaged in the construction of a gabledroof house came to one of the authors of this book to ask a question in mathematics. The following conversation took place:

Builder. I want to know how to find the angle at which to pitch a gabled roof. 

Author. That's simple. Do you know the rise? (See figure.) 1

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

Builder. Yes. Eight feet. 

Author. What is the run or the span? (See figure.)

Builder. The span is 24 feet, so that the run must be 

12 feet.

Author (taking a trigonometry book from his bookshelf). I convert the fraction into the decimal .67 and consult this 

Table of Trigonometric Functions. The angle at which you are to pitch the roof is approximately 34°. 

Courtesy, Tennessee Valley Authority

ROAD INCLINATION 

It required a high degree of precision to fit the bridge to the inclination of the roadway.

The author was able to answer the question asked by the builder because he knew the dependence of one part of a triangle upon other parts. Other instances in which this dependence is used are found in the calculation of distances across swamps, lakes, forests, and inaccessible places in general. This interdependence of the parts of a triangle is also used to find the heights of tall buildings or trees, the distances of ships at sea or of objects on a landscape, the altitude of the sun and of other stars. We may summarize by stating that for measurements which must be found indirectly, trigonometry is basic.

TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

3 Besides the practical uses of trigonometry mentioned in the previous paragraph, and its obvious use in surveying and navigation, there is its indispensable use in mathematical analysis. 

Parts of analytical geometry and of the calculus would be impossible to understand without a knowledge of plane trigonometry. 

Certain work in the solution of equations is greatly facilitated by the use of trigonometry in higher mathematics. To some people this use of trigonometry in mathematical analysis is of prime importance.

In plane trigonometry you will study the interesting relations of the sides and of the angles of triangles and of figures that can be decomposed into triangles. When you learn and understand these relations, you will better appreciate what is meant when we say that the sides and the angles of a triangle are interrelated.

Three important ratios. Let us begin the study of these relations by examining the right triangle ABC in which angle C is the right angle.

In the right triangle ABC, we call the hypotenuse, the side opposite the right angle, c, and the two legs, a and b. To distinguish between a and b, we speak of the opposite leg and the adjacent leg, depending upon the angle to which we refer. 

If we refer to angle A, then b is the adjacent leg and a, the opposite leg. If we refer to angle B, then b is the opposite, and 

4, the adjacent leg.

The sine of the right angle C will be explained more fully later.

Definition of trigonometric functions. The three ratios, bab and others to be studied later are referred to as the c' c' a' 

trigonometric functions of an angle. In elementary mathematics a function may be defined as an expression in which the value of one quantity depends upon the value of another. For example, the circumference of a circle is a function of the radius since its value depends upon the length of its radius. 

The number of books you can buy for ten dollars depends upon the cost of the individual books. Can you discover other illustrations of functional relationships in everyday life or in the sciences?

It is easily seen that the value of the sine of an angle depends upon the size of the angle. Consequently, the sine of an angle is a function of the angle. In a similar manner, the cosine and the tangent of an angle are functions. Of what?

C b Referring back to the right triangle, let us assume that b = 5 inches and a = 12 inches. Then AB can be found by the

Pythagorean Theorem which you have already studied in geometry: 

The square of the hypotenuse of a right triangle is equal to the square of one leg plus the square of the other leg. 

Corollary. The square of either leg is equal to the square of the hypotenuse diminished by the square of the other leg. 

Hence c² = 52 + 122 169 and, therefore, c = 13. By the definitions given above, cos A = 

13, or .38* in decimal form. Find in decimal form the values of cos A and tan A. 

Also find sin B, cos B, and tan B. What discovery have you made?

VALUES OF TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

A table of trigonometric ratios. Since the trigonometric ratios or functions depend only on given angles, it is possible to obtain these ratios for certain angles once so that they can be used for reference whenever needed. You will find such a table beginning on page 216, where the functions are given to four decimal places. It is headed "Values of Trigonometric 

Functions." Learn how to use it. The following instructions will help. 

How to use the tables. To find sin 23°. Look in the extreme left column headed angle. Find 23°. To the right under the heading sin you will find .3907; that is, sin 23° 0.3907. 

To find cos 52°. Look in the extreme right column. Find 

52°. Four columns to the left, over the word cos you will find 

0.6157. .. cos 52° 0.6157. 

To find tan 70°. Look in the extreme right column. Find 

70°. One column to the left you will find 2.7475. .. tan 70° = 2.7475.

To find the angle whose cosine is .9397. Look in column three under the heading cos for .9397. To the left of this number you will find 20°. The angle whose cosine is .9397 is 20°. 

To find the angle whose cosine is .6018. This you will find in column two over the word cos. To the right you find 53°.

The angle whose cosine is 0.6018 is 53°.

Your own tables. To get a better understanding of this topic, try to construct a small table of trigonometric functions. 

Construct a right triangle making one of the acute angles 30°. 

Measure as accurately as you can the opposite leg and the hypotenuse. Divide the first number by the second. Your answer should be very close to .50, i.e., sin 30° = .50. Measure the other leg and divide the length of the shorter leg by that of the longer leg. Your answer should be .58, i.e., tan 30° = .58. Find, in a similar manner, cos 30°. Do the same for the other acute angle which is 60°. Find sin 60°, cos 60°, tan 60°. 

Construct right triangles with other acute angles. Compute their sines, cosines, and tangents. 

Note. This method of computing functions is not the method used in the preparation of the table "Values of Trigonometric Functions." Your method is too laborious and not sufficiently accurate. Instead, theorems in higher mathematics are used; these you may study at some future time,

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

A worthwhile discovery. Co-functions. Did you notice while doing the previous exercises that sin 10° was the same as cos 

80°? that sin 25° = cos 65°? cos 58° = sin 32°? sin 58° cos 32°? 10° and 80° are, as you know, complementary angles. 

So are 25° and 65°; 58° and 32°. We shall, therefore, say that when we have complementary angles, the sine of one angle is always equal to the cosine of the other. Does this statement explain why sin 45° = cos 45°? Prove sin 45° = cos 45° in another way.

Prove the following statement directly from the figure in which a and b are the legs and c, the hypotenuse of a right triangle.

Important theorem. When two angles are complementary, the sine of one angle equals the cosine of the other. 

Additional ratios. In addition to the sine, cosine, and tangent of an angle, there are three other ratios that are useful. The cotangent of an angle is the ratio of the adjacent leg to the opposite leg. For example, cot B is cot A? opp. leg adj. leg a What

The cosecant of an angle is the ratio of the hypotenuse to the opposite leg.

CSC B = hyp. opp. leg 

What is csc A? Important theorem. When two angles are complementary, the tangent of one angle equals the cotangent of the other; the secant of one angle equals the cosecant of the other. 

Note. These theorems explain why, in the table, it is not necessary to work out functions for angles between 45° and 90° if the functions of angles between 0° and 45° have already been obtained.

Reciprocal functions. A brief examination of the six functions of angle A in right triangle ABC will make the following formulas clear. Substituting for each function the appropriate ratio of two sides of the triangle, we obtain six important relations. 

From our definitions,

Four other functions. There are four other functions, the versed sine, the coversed sine, the suversed sine, and the haversed sine. These four, however, are relatively unimportant in analytical work. They are used in special engineering and surveying work.

Since the values of the trigonometric functions as shown in the Table of  are given for angles subdivided into ten minute intervals, the question must have already occurred to you: "How do I find, for example, sin 36° 23' or how do I find the angle whose sine is .5932?"

These questions are best answered by illustrative examples. 

AFAYETTE INSTRUMENTS NEW YORK, NEW YORK BUBBLE ATTACHMENT 

Courtesy, Lafayette Instrument Company

THE SEXTANT 

The sextant is used to measure angles with a high degree of accuracy. 

It is more elaborate and more useful than the angle mirror. It is indispensable in traveling whether by ship or by airplane. 

Example 1. Find sin 36° 23'.

Solution. It is reasonable to assume that since 36° 23' is between 36° 30' and 36° 20', the sine of 36° 23' will be between the values of the sines for 36° 30' and 36° 20' respectively. 

Although the difference between the angles is not theoretically proportional to the difference between their sines, the assumption that it is so, is sufficiently accurate for our purposes. 

The following table will help to arrange the process clearly

SIMPLE IDENTITIES 

In working examples 11-20 on page 22 were you sufficiently alert to note that certain relations are always true, others are only sometimes true, while still others are never true? For example, the equality sin x = cos x is true if x 45°, but untrue 30° or 60°. However, the equality cot² A + 1 = csc² A if x = is always true, no matter what the value of A is. 

An equation that is true for all values of the variable is called an identical equation, or, briefly, an identity. 

Example. Prove that sin2 x sin² y = cos² y cos2 x. 

Proof

An inspection of this identity evidently involves the use of formula 7(a) on page 13. 

(1) sin² x + cos² x (2) sin² y + (3) .. sin² x cos² y + cos² x = (4) .. sin² x sin2 y 

1 1 Formula 7(a) Formula 7(a) sin2 y+cos2 y cos2 y Axiom cos² x Transposition

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY APPROXIMATE NUMBERS 

Measurement. All measurement is more or less inexact. 

For some purposes very rough measurements are satisfactory; for others, measurements must be very precise or highly refined. 

A pupil who measured the length of his schoolroom reported to the class that he found the length to be 18 feet. Thereupon, the teacher explained that according to this report, the true length of the room is between 17.5 feet and 18.5 feet. This is usually written (18.5) feet. If the pupil had used a surveyor's tape which is divided into tenths of a foot, he might have reported the length of the room as 17.8 feet. This, of course, would mean that the true length is between 17.75 feet and 17.85 feet or (17.8 ± .05) feet. From these and a few more illustrations, the pupils discovered the following rule: 

A true measurement is actually within the limits of one-half unit more and one-half unit less than the observed measurement. 

Write the true measurements for the following observed measurements: 

3.24", 7.2', 26.65 cm., 0.87". 

The answers are, respectively, (3.24 ± .005)', (7.2 ± .05)', (26.65.005) centimeters, (0.87 ± .005)'.

Significant digits. Even though an observed measurement of 18 feet varies within the limits of 17.5 feet and 18.5 feet, we, nevertheless, know that the true measurement is nearer to 18 feet than to 17 feet or to 19 feet. In other words, the digits 1 and 8 in the number 18 both have meaning or significance as a measurement. They are, therefore, called significant figures, digits, or numbers. It is true that the 8 is not a sure or certain digit as the 1 is; nevertheless, it has significance in conveying to us the information that the true measure is nearer to 18 feet than to any other number of feet. 

In a measurement like 17.8 feet there are three significant figures. A measurement of 17.0 feet also has three significant

TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

25 ures. figures. However, 0.025 inches has only two significant fig- 

Zeros are therefore sometimes significant and sometimes not significant. When we say that the distance from the earth to the sun is 93,000,000 miles, none of the zeros is significant. 

Zeros such as these and such as the first two in 0.025 inches merely help to locate the decimal point correctly. Where doubt may arise about the significance of zeros, it is advisable to use the notation employed by the scientist. If, for example, the number 24,000 enters into a scientific statement and it is known that there are three significant figures in this number, the scientist will write 2.40 X 10. The exponential system of notation has the advantage of showing at a glance the exact number of significant figures. 

Use the following rules to determine the number of significant figures: 

1. The numbers from 1 to 9 are always significant. 

2. Zero is significant when it represents the degree of precision of an observed or calculated value. 

3. Zero is significant when it appears between two other significant figures.. 

4. Zero is not significant when it serves merely to locate the decimal point and does not represent precision of measurement or calculation. 

5. When every figure of a number is correct except the last, and that contains an error of one-half unit or less in its place, all the figures of the number are said to be significant. 

How many significant figures are there in the following measurements: 

3.24", 4.202 centimeters, 26.65 feet, 0.87", 6.00 feet?

The answers are, respectively, three, four, four, two, three. 

Rounding off. When it is desired to add, subtract, multiply, or divide measurements, we should, if at all possible, work with numbers representing the same degree of precision. If, however, there are measurements of varying degrees of precision, it is highly advisable first to reduce them all to the same degree

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

of precision. (See the rules given later on.) This means that we must reduce the number of significant figures in the more precise measurements. The reduction should be done in accordance with the following rule:

If the figure following the one we are going to retain is 5, 6, 7, 8, or 9, then change the last retained figure to one more and drop all the remaining figures.

If the figure following the one we are going to retain is 0, 1, 2, 3, or 4, then do not change the last retained figure. 

For example, to round off 26.65" to three significant figures, we write 26.7". Rounding off 6.0 feet to one significant figure 

Notice that rounding off makes a measurement gives 6 feet. less precise.

Results in operations with approximate numbers.

General rule. No figure except zero, used to locate the decimal point, should be retained if it is not significant. That

TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

27 is, in any number every digit but the last should be exactly correct, and the last should contain an error of not more than one half the value of a unit in that place. 

ADDITION AND SUBTRACTION 

Example 1. Find the perimeter of a triangle whose sides are 7.34", 12.93", and 9.55". 7.34 12.93 9.55 29.82 

The perimeter is therefore 29.82", although there may be an error in the last place. 

Example 2. Find the difference in length between the sides of a parallelogram whose base is 1.432 cm. and whose adjacent side is 0.987 cm. 1.432 0.987 0.445 

The difference is 0.445 cm., although there may be an error in the last place.

Example 3. Find the perimeter of a quadrilateral whose sides are 61.34 feet, 52.186 feet, 70.08 feet, and 59.249 feet. (This type of data involving different degrees of precision should be avoided.) 61.34 70.08 131.42 52.186 59.249 111.435 131.42 111.44 242.86 

The perimeter is 242.86 feet, although there may be an error in the last place.

RULES FOR ADDITION AND SUBTRACTION OF APPROXIMATE NUMBERS 

1. If the numbers are all of the same degree of precision, add them as in Example 1, if it appears reasonable that the

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

errors in the measurements cancel each other. If it is known definitely that the errors are cumulative, drop the last significant figure in the answer; in integers, replace the digit dropped by a zero. 

2. If the numbers are not all of the same degree of precision, first find the separate sums of those addends that have the same degree of precision, reduce the more precise sums to the less precise ones, and add as in rule 1. (See Example 3.) 

3. The rules for subtraction are the same. (See Example 2.)

Note on π. When working with the number use as many significant figures as conform with the rules here given for addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division of approximate numbers.

MULTIPLICATION AND DIVISION Example 1. Find the area of a rectangle whose dimensions are 2.43" and 5.61". 2.43 X 5.61 

13.6323 The area is 13.6 sq. in.

Example 2. Find the area of a rectangle whose base is 48.7' 

and whose altitude is 29.3'. 48.7 X 29.3 

1426.91 The area is 1430 sq. ft.

Note that this product is correct to three significant figures. 

Example 3. The base of a parallelogram is 3.39" and its altitude is 3.02". Find the area. 3.39 X 3.02 

10.2378 The area is 10 sq. in., if the errors are cumulative. 

The area is 10.2 sq. in., if the errors cancel each other.

29 Example 4. Find the value of a force which is equal to 

5.2 cos 29° lb. 

5.2 is to be multiplied by cos 29° which is .8746. Therefore, round off .8746 to .875. 5.2 X 0.875 

4.55 The answer is 4.6 lb.

Example 5. Find the altitude of a parallelogram if its base is 3.7 feet and its area is 6.139 square feet. 6.140 3.7 = 1.66 The altitude is 1.7 feet.

RULES FOR MULTIPLICATION AND DIVISION OF APPROXIMATE NUMBERS 

1. If two approximate numbers have the same degree of precision, their product is in general correct to the same number of significant figures. (See Examples 1 and 2.) Occasionally, as in Example 3, the number of figures to be retained may be one less than the number in the factors. 

2. If one of the approximate numbers is more precise than the other, round off the more precise number to one significant place more than the less precise number (See Example 4.) 

3. The rules for division are the same (See Example 5.)

To solve a triangle is to compute the unknown parts when three parts, of which at least one is a side, are given. The method of procedure will be best understood by studying the following illustrative examples. 

Example 1. Given a triangle in which B and c

Solution 1. The problem may be solved by drawing a figure as accurately as possible with the given information, and then obtaining the results by measuring the desired parts. If a rough approximation is all that is needed, this method may be employed to advantage. Greater accuracy is obtained by skilled workers using instruments with a high degree of precision. What is meant by the expression, "instruments with a high degree of precision"?

If the data are such that it is not convenient to draw a triangle with the given measurements, then the triangle should be drawn to scale, and the unknown sides computed. The scale should be indicated in the diagram. To measure the angles, use a protractor. With the ordinary protractor, it will not be possible to read the angles more accurately than to the nearest half of a degree, or thirty minutes.

THE TRANSIT 

The transit is used, when sighting objects, to find angles of elevation or angles of depression. It is also used for finding horizontal angles. The transit shown here, used by surveyors and engineers, is equipped with special devices to increase the precision and accuracy of the angle readings. the object. In this case, the object is below and not above the horizontal line. The difference in the name of the angle arises only from the position of the object with reference to the observer.

Example 1. Two forces of 7 lbs. and 28 lbs., at right angles to each other, are pulling on an object. Find the resultant of these forces. Also find the direction of the resultant. 

The resultant of two forces is a single force that has the same effect as the two combined forces. 

When a line represents both magnitude and direction, it is called a vector.

Example 2. A 180-lb. barrel is supported on an inclined plane by a rope parallel to the plane. If the inclination of the plane is 19°, find the pull on the rope and the pressure on the plane.

180 lbs.

Vector BP represents the pressure of the barrel on the plane. Vector PW therefore represents the force necessary to hold the barrel on the inclined plane, since BW is the resultant of BP and PW.

CHAPTER III TRIGONOMETRIC  

Perhaps your curiosity is sufficiently aroused to ask, "Is it possible to find sin 219°, tan 300°, or cos 522°?" Certainly we cannot construct right triangles in which an angle is 91°, 172°, or 219°. If functions of such angles are to exist, it will be necessary for us to expand some of our very elementary ideas concerning angles.

In 1637 René Descartes, a French philosopher and mathematician, gave to the world an invaluable invention, — graphing by coordinate axes. With his plan we can find the trigonometric function of angles larger than the acute angles you have been studying. 

Coordinate axes. Draw two axes XX' and YY' perpendicular to each other dividing the plane into four quadrants. These are numbered as shown in the accompanying figure. The point of intersection O is called the origin. We call XX' the axis of abscissas; YY' the axis of ordinates. Lengths measured along XX' to the right of O are positive, while those to the left of O are negative. Lengths measured along YY' above

O are positive, while those measured below O are negative. 

To locate a point in the plane, we need two numbers of reference called coordinates: the first number is the abscissa, the second is the ordinate. Both numbers may be positive; both, negative; one may be negative and the other positive.

If both are positive, the point lies in quadrant I; if both are negative, in quadrant III. If the abscissa is positive and the ordinate is negative, the point is in quadrant IV. If the abscissa is negative and the ordinate is positive, the point is in quadrant II. 49

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

Example. Locate the point (2, 3). This point is 2 units to the right of O along OX and 3 units below O along OY'. It is therefore in quadrant IV, as shown in the diagram.

Courtesy, Columbia University Library RENÉ DESCARTES 

René Descartes (1596-1650) was a gentleman, soldier, and mathematician. In 1637 he presented to the world his invention of coordinate axes whereby we use algebra for the discovery and investigation of geometrical theorems. This invention ranks among the greatest in the world. 

Positive and negative angles. To form angle AOA', it is necessary to rotate a line from its initial position OA counter-

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

clockwise until it reaches the position OA' as shown by the arrow. OA is the initial side of angle AOA' and OA' is the terminal side.

Α' Y' We can obtain the same result by rotating OA' clockwise until it reaches the position OA as shown in the diagram. In this case OA' is the initial side and OA, the terminal side. We agree to call angles formed by a counterclockwise rotation positive angles; those formed by a clockwise rotation, negative angles. 

So far you have studied the positive angle with the terminal side in the first quadrant. The annexed three drawings show positive angles with terminal sides in quadrants II, III, and IV. -X X· AX -X X²

The following four diagrams show negative angles in the four quadrants.

From these diagrams you can easily verify the fact that every positive angle is equivalent to some other negative angle. 

A positive angle of 40° is equivalent to a negative angle of 320°. 

Give more illustrations.

Α' Α' BO A. -XX -X X- -X B Ο Α' Α' Y' Trigonometric functions broadly defined.

We can now re define the trigonometric functions as follows: 

The sine of an angle is the ratio of the ordinate to the distance from the origin. For example, the sine of angle AOA' in any one quadrant is the ordinate of point A' divided by the distance OA', i.e., sin AOA' =

The cosine ordinate distance BA' OA' of an angle is the ratio of the abscissa to the distance, i.e.

, cos AOA' abscissa OB distance OA' The tangent is the ratio of the ordinate to the abscissa, e.g., ordinate BA' tan AOA' abscissa OB The cotangent is the ratio of the abscissa to the ordinate, e.g.

, abscissa OB cot AOA' ordinate BA'

The secant is the ratio of the distance to the abscissa, e.g., sec AOA' distance OA' abscissa OB

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

The cosecant is the ratio of the distance to the ordinate, e.g.

, distance OA' CSC AOA' ordinate BA'

The distance of the point from the origin is always taken as a positive line. The ordinate and abscissa, however, may be positive or negative. Accordingly, the trigonometric functions become positive or negative depending upon the quadrant in which the terminal side lies, and care must be exercised to give the functions the proper sign.

To illustrate. If the terminal side is in the first quadrant, the ordinate is positive and so is the abscissa. Therefore the sine, cosine, and tangent are all positive. So are the reciprocal functions, the cosecant, the secant, and the cotangent. 

If the terminal side is in the third quadrant, the ordinate is negative and so is the abscissa. Therefore, the sine and cosine are negative numbers, but the tangent is positive. Why?

Since the reciprocal functions will, of course, have the same signs, the cosecant and secant are negative, while the cotangent is positive. Similarly for the other quadrants. 

The following table will help you remember these relations.

LINE FUNCTIONS 

In order to understand more easily the relation between functions of positive and those of negative angles and also to facilitate the graphing of the six trigonometric ratios it becomes necessary once more to modify our definitions of the trigonometric functions.

The unit circle. Let us call the radius of circle O one unit.

Variations. These line functions are a splendid device for studying variations in the magnitudes of the six trigonometric - functions. 

As angle AOA' increases from 0° to 90°, BA' grows from a length of zero to a length of one unit (the radius of the circle), while OB decreases from one unit to zero. This conclusion agrees with our previous one that as an angle increases from 0° to 90° its sine increases from 0 to 1 and its cosine decreases from 1 to 0.

In like manner it can be shown that as an angle increases from 0 to 90° the tangent grows from 0 to ∞o while the cotangent decreases from ∞ to 0.

Similarly, the secant increases from 1 to ∞ while the cosecant decreases from ∞ to 1.

Work out the variations in magnitude of the functions of an angle that increases from 90° to 180°; an angle that increases from 180° to 270°; finally, an angle that increases from 

270° to 360°.

REDUCTION FORMULAS 

To find the numerical values of the trigonometric functions of angles greater than 90° it is not necessary to have special tables. We can use the Table of Values of Trigonometric 

Functions we already have, because every angle greater than

90° has a "mate" less than 90° whose respective functions are numerically the same. For example, cos 240° and cos 60° are numerically the same. It is true that cos 240° is negative while cos 60° is positive, but the absolute values of the cosines, the values without the signs, are the same. 

Study the following illustrative examples to find out how the acute angle “mate” is determined for any angle, positive or negative. Also study the "Summary of Important Rela tions" known as reduction formulas.

CHAPTER IV  

Trigonometry is so much concerned with the measurement of angles that it is wise to re-examine now the subject of angle measurement. 

All measurement is in terms of units. Explain in your own words what this statement means. How are units of measurement determined? 

In what units are angles measured? You will probably answer "in degrees." But this answer is only partially correct; we employ several units which measure angles. 

Degrees. The unit with which most people are familiar is the degree. This unit is satisfactory for the practical applications of trigonometry. Two other smaller units are associated with the degree, namely, the minute and the second. 

The following table shows how these units are related: 

60 seconds (60″) = 1 minute (1') 

60 minutes (60') = 1 degree (1°) 

1 right angle. 

90 degrees (90°) Radians. In the higher branches of mathematics, the degree is an inconvenient unit. We employ another unit known as the radian. To understand what a radian is, study the accompanying diagram. Circle O is constructed with radius r. 

Let the length of arc AB (not chord AB) be equal to the radius r. Connect A and B with O forming the central angle 

AOB. Angle AOB is one radian. This may be written 1". 70

This unit is a constant quantity; it does not depend upon the particular circle used. Can you prove this statement?

1 radian → Definition. A radian is a central angle whose arc is equal in length to the radius of the circle. 

Converting degrees into radians. 

Example. Given an angle of 70°. Find how many radians it contains. To do this, we must first discover how many degrees there are in one radian. The circumference of a circle is equal to 

2 times the radius. That is, c = 2 r. The radius will therefore be contained in the circumference exactly 2 times. 

Another way of expressing the same idea is to say that the circumference can be divided into 2 arcs each equal to the radius. For every such arc, there will be subtended at the center an angle of one radian. 2π arcs will subtend 2 π radians. Since the total number of degrees about the center of a circle is 360°, 2 π radians 360°. From this it follows that 

360° 180° 1 radian 

2 π which is 57° 17' 45", approximately.

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

It also follows that 1 degree 

2 π 360 radians or 180 Therefore, to change an angle of 70° into radians we multiply π" 70 by 180 70 X 7π radians. 180 18 If we substitute for the fraction 22, the answer will be 12 radians. Rule. 

To transform degrees into radians multiply the number of degrees by 180 

Converting radians into degrees. 

Example. Given an angle of 2 radians. Find the number of degrees it contains. 180° 360° 2 X By substituting the value 22 for π, we can write the answer as 114° 33', approximately. By substituting the value 3.1416 for T, we can write the answer as 114° 35', approximately, depending upon the degree of accuracy desired. 

This process is the inverse to the preceding one. The following rule will appear obvious, since 1 radian = 180° Rule.

To change an angle from radians to degrees multiply the number of radians by 180° Caution.

Angles are often given in radian units with the symbol for radians omitted, e.g.

, means an angle of radians equivalent to 60°. Remember that equals 3.1416 approximately and that an angle of π radians is equivalent to 180°.

CHAPTER V GRAPHS OF TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

Algebra is largely concerned with a study of relations between varying quantities. How does algebra achieve this purpose?

Mainly by means of the formula, the equation, and the graph. 

In a similar manner, we study variation in the trigonometric functions by means of the formula, the equation, and the graph. 

We have already learned something about trigonometric formulas and trigonometric equations. We shall now examine the graphs of the trigonometric functions; they are especially effective for studying changes. 

Graph of y = sin x. sin x. Let us begin with the graph of the sine function. To plot the curve or the locus of the equation y = sin x, we construct a table of values.

Does the curve have one maximum point? One minimum point? Is the curve finite or infinite in length? Does it possess symmetry? Does it resemble any of the conic sections? 

Does the curve repeat itself? The interval of repetition is called a period of symmetry. The sine function is called a periodic function with each period equal to 2 π radians. The height of the maximum ordinate (or minimum ordinate) is called the amplitude. The curve y sin x has an amplitude of 1. Graph of y = cos x. Use the same plan employed in plotting the sine curve.

There is a strong temptation to suppose that sin (x + y) is equal to sin x + sin y, and similarly for the other functions. 

It is possible to find special angles for which this relation is true. In general, however, if the angles x and y are taken at random, sin (x + y) does not equal sin x + sin y.

CHAPTER VII LOGARITHMS 

Labor-saving devices are as old as the human race itself. 

Inventors are constantly seeking ways to make laborious tasks less irksome. Logarithms were invented for the purpose of saving time and energy when extensive computations are involved. 

JOHN NAPIER Courtesy, Columbia Library 

John Napier (1550-1617) invented the natural logarithms whose base is e = 2.71828 . . . . For ordinary computational work 10 is taken as the base. This system was subsequently invented by Henry Briggs (1561-1631). 102

LOGARITHMS 

103 Nowadays, we use calculating machines for the purpose of saving labor, but in the days of John Napier and Henry Briggs, the two inventors of logarithms, calculating machines were unknown, or, at best, only little known.

The invention known as logarithms is a simple one. Let us see how it works. 

You learned in algebra that a4 a3 a7. Likewise, 24. 23 = 27 or 128. To multiply the two numbers 24 and 2³, it is only necessary to add their exponents, and their sum is made the exponent of the same base, 2, in the product. If, then, we wish to multiply 64 by 256, we change 64 into a power of 2 and 256 into a power of 2, add the exponents, and express the result as a new power of 2. We have 64 X 256 26 X 28 214.

If, now, we have a table of the powers of 2 prepared in advance, we can look up the value of 2¹4 and find that the result is 16,384. Similarly, you have learned that a ÷ a² = a¹, or 28 ÷ 2³ = 25.

23 Consequently, to divide the number 28 by the number 2³, it is only necessary to subtract the second exponent from the first and express the result as a new exponent of 2. If we wish to divide 4096 by 256, we change both numbers to powers of 2, subtract the second exponent from the first, and look up the resulting power of 2 in the table. 

We have, 4096 212 

24 16. 256 28 The important thing to remember is that the numbers you wish to multiply or divide must be reduced to powers of the same base; in this case, the base 2. 

To do the following examples, first prepare a table of powers of 2, as far as 22º, as follows: 2⁰ 1 26 64 211 2048 216 21 22 23 2 27 128 212 217 ? 28 213 218 = ? 29 = ? 24 16 210 ? 214 215 219 ? ? 220 25 32

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

Proceed in the following manner: (1) Change each number to a power of 2. (2) If you are multiplying, add the exponents. (3) If you are dividing, subtract the exponent of the divisor from that of the dividend. (4) Look up the resulting power of 2 in the table.

Do we strike a snag? The question naturally arises, "What happens when the numbers we wish to multiply or divide cannot be expressed as exact powers of 2, such as 237 × 595 or 

1203 ÷ 454?" Then, we have to use approximate powers of 2. 

How are these approximate powers of 2 worked out? By methods that belong to higher mathematics which you will probably elect in your college courses. These methods are too difficult for you to understand at the present stage of your mathematical education. Nevertheless, we can use the results obtained for us by mathematicians, even though we cannot fully understand now how the results are obtained.

LOGARITHMS 

105 To multiply 237 by 595, it is, therefore, necessary to change 237 and 595 into powers of 2. Since these numbers are not exact powers of 2, they would have to be expressed as approximate powers of 2. But why express them as powers of 2 at all? Why not write them as powers of 3, 4, 10, or some other number?

With the exception of zero and one, any number may be selected as the base in which to express the given numbers. 

Why can we not use 1 as a base? Since we have such a wide choice of numbers that may be used as a base, we choose that number which is most convenient for computations, and that number is 10. 10-1 10-2 10-3 10-4 

1 10 1 

CHARACTERISTIC AND MANTISSA 

100 1 .1 10º 

1 104 10,000 .01 101 10 105 100,000 .001 102 100 106 1,000,000 

.0001 103 1000 10,000 1000 1 You can now express any positive real number as a power of ten. 

Example. Express 237 as a power of 10. 66 The number 237 falls between 100 and 1000. It is, therefore, between 102 and 103. Consequently, 237 102+, where the "+" represents a fraction or a decimal less than one, since this exponent 2+ is more than two and less than three. 

Similarly 1203 falls between 1000 and 10,000. It may be written as 10³+. The same procedure may be followed for any number which is not an exact power of ten. 

In general, when a number is written as a power of 10, the exponent will consist of two parts, an integral part and a decimal part. The part of the exponent to the left of the decimal

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

point (the whole part) is called the characteristic. The part of the exponent to the right of the decimal point (the fractional part) is called the mantissa.

To express a number as a power of 10, it is, therefore, necessary first to determine the characteristic and secondly to find the mantissa of the exponent or logarithm. Bear in mind that a logarithm is an exponent. 

How to find the mantissa. (1) Turn to the Table of Man tissas beginning on page 212, called Logarithms of Numbers. (2) Look in the column headed "N" for the first two digits of the number. If the number is 237, look for 23 in the "N" column. Eight columns to the right is a column headed “7.” (3) The number at the intersection of the row marked "23" and the column headed "7" is the mantissa you are looking for. It is .3747. Bear in mind that these tables record mantissas only. Therefore, 237 = 102.3747.

QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS 

Q. Can every real number be represented as a power of 10?

A. Yes. 

Q. Will the exponent of the base ten always be an integer? 

A. No.

Q. If the exponent is not an integer, how do we find what the exponent is?

A. We use a Table of Logarithms, or a Table of Mantissas.

The relation between involution, evolution, and logarithms. 

The following illustration will show the relation between involution, evolution, and logarithmation. (1) Involution, 53 = ? ?³ (2) Evolution, ?3 = 125 (3) Logarithmation, 5 = 125 (1) The process of finding the answer to 53 ? is called involution. 5³ = 5·5·5 = 125. 125 is called a power of 5. 53 You were given the base and the logarithm and you found the power. (2) The process of finding the answer to (?)3 = 125 is called evolution. It answers the question, What number raised to the third power gives 125, or, what is the cube root of 125? The answer is 5. 5, the base, is the cube root of 125. (3) The process of finding the answer to 5 = 125 is called logarithmation. 5 raised to what exponent or logarithm gives 

125? The answer is 3. 3 is called the logarithm of 125 to the base 5. It is written, log, 125 = 3. When the base is 10 it is usually omitted. For example, log 10,000 = 4. It means that 104 10,000. An important relation. Since the smallest number whose mantissa can be found in the Table of Mantissas is 100, we must discover a relation for numbers between 1 and 100 with those between 100 and 999. This can be understood best by studying the following illustration:

Example 1. To find log 23.7. 

Since 23.7

When you want the mantissa of a number less than 100, disregard the decimal point in the number. The characteristic is determined in the usual manner.

20. Find log 6.72 + log 49.8. Logs of numbers less than one. From the table of the powers of 10 on page 104, you see that 10° = 1, or that log 1 (base 10) = 0 since 10° 1. Consequently, the logarithm of a number less than one, will be less than zero and, therefore, negative. But in actual practice, for the sake of convenience, it is only the characteristic of the logarithm that becomes negative while the mantissa remains positive. Since only part of the logarithm is negative, while the other part is positive, we cannot place the minus sign in front of the whole logarithm. 

Therefore, we resort to one of the following devices:

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

(1) We place a minus sign above the characteristic. For example, log 0.237 = 1.3747. It means, 1.3747. 

(2) We add 10 to and subtract 10 from the logarithm and write the result in simplified form. For example, log 0.237 9.3747 - 10. 

This is obtained as follows: 

- 1 + .3747 10 - 1 + .3747 - 10 9.3747 - 10 

9.3747 10.

METHODS OF DETERMINING THE CHARACTERISTIC OF A LOGARITHM 

Method 1. Refer to a table of powers of 10. 

Method 2. If the number is 10 or more than 10, the characteristic may be found by counting the digits to the left of the units' digit. Of course, the characteristic will then be positive. 

If the number is a positive decimal less than 1, count the decimal places to the right of the units' digit, which is zero, to include only the first significant figure after the decimal point. 

Be sure to make this characteristic negative.

Method 3. For numbers greater than 1, the characteristic is one less than the number of digits in the integral part of the number, and is positive. For positive numbers less than 1, the characteristic is one more than the number of zeros between the decimal point and the first significant figure, and is negative.

INTERPOLATION 

You are already familiar with the reason for the need of interpolation. Just as it was necessary to interpolate in the table of natural functions for sin 32° 17', because the table is arranged in ten-minute intervals, so it is necessary to interpolate for the mantissas of the logarithms of numbers like 

2752 and 0.06637.

The results obtained by the process of interpolation are approximately correct. Nevertheless, they are sufficiently accurate for our purposes.

The student should verify these statements from the graph of the function x = logo y which is also the graph of the function y = 10. From the graph try to determine whether the interpolations will be of equal accuracy throughout.

Antilogarithms. In the preceding sections, we learned how to find the logarithm of a number. The inverse process, known

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

as finding an antilogarithm, is the process of finding the number that corresponds to a given logarithm. The difference between the two processes can be understood from the following illustrations:

Example 1. Find the antilogarithm of 1.7513. This means, of course, the number whose logarithm is 1.7513. The two parts of the logarithm are therefore given. 

The characteristic of this logarithm is 1. 

The mantissa of this logarithm is .7513. 

Find .7513 in the table of mantissas. Read the number corresponding to this mantissa. It is 564.

We must now locate the decimal point correctly. Since 1.7513 is between 1.0000 and 2.0000, 101.7513 is between 101.8000 and 

102.0000. Therefore, 101.7513 is between 10 and 100.

The decimal point, therefore, belongs between the 4 and the 6. .. antilog 1.7513 = 56.4.

Computations with logarithms. 

Example 1. Compute the value of 93.7 × 177 × 6.25³.

In most problems, and especially in logarithmic computation, we first make a rough estimate of the answer. This is done to avoid gross errors. Flagrant errors are usually the result of misplacing the decimal point. From a practical point of view, if the correct answer to a problem is 9.2, it is better to obtain an answer of 9 than an answer of 92. 

Rough estimate. 93.7 X 177 X 6.25³ 100 200 - 216 = 4,320,000. 

Our answer should be about 4 million.

In order to understand the logarithmic solution more easily, we will first do this problem exponentially.

Some engineers, when working with involved quotients, prefer to add the cologarithm of the divisor to the logarithm of the dividend. Adding the cologarithm of a number is equivalent to subtracting the logarithm of the number. 

An illustration will make the meaning of cologarithms clear: 

The logarithm of 2 is 0.3010 

The cologarithm of 2 is 9.6990 - 10

This is obtained in the following manner:

Equations in which the unknown letter is an exponent are called exponential equations. In simple cases, the solution can be found by reducing both sides of the equation to the same base, and then equating the exponents. Thus, 2x 64 2x = 26 .. x = 6. Why?

LOGARITHMS OF TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

Most of the computations employed in trigonometry involve trigonometric functions. A thorough knowledge of the logarithms you have already studied will make the transition to trigonometric functions easy.

To save time and to facilitate the necessary computations, we use the table headed Logarithms of Trigonometric Functions.

How to use the table. Suppose, in a problem, there appears sin 53°. Instead of first finding the natural sin 53°, which is .7986, and then working out the logarithm of this number, we use the table which gives us directly the logarithm of the sine of 53°. 

Example 1. To find log sin 53°. Look for 53° in the column headed "angle." Under the column headed "log sin" we find 

9.9023. This is really 9.9023 10, for the sine of an angle cannot exceed the number 1. The 10 is omitted to save 

space, .. log sin 53° 9.9023 

10. sin 53°. 

This means 109.9023–10 If the angle does not appear in the table, such as cos 32° 43', we resort to interpolation.

THE SLIDE RULE 

log tan x The slide rule is an instrument used for calculations involving multiplication, division, proportion, square and cube root.

Results with the ten-inch rule are correct to three significant figures, which, with a few exceptional cases, is equivalent to a three-place log table. In addition to its three-place accuracy, the rule is useful as a rough check on calculations involving more than three figures.

LOG 2C1 2 -LOG 8Courtesy, Keuffel & Esser

Most practical problems in science and industry are sufficiently accurate when carried to three figures. The use of the rule saves much time in the calculations of business and industry.

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

Manuals with instructions for learning to use the rule are issued by the makers of slide rules. Anyone who knows decimal fractions can learn to operate the slide rule. A3π

2 2 11 12 12 14 15 16 2 

KEUFFEL & ESSER CO N.Y. 

Indicator 

Courtesy, Keuffel & Esser

In the trigonometric solution of triangles, the slide rule provides an additional check by the use of the Law of Sines. 

With more complicated rules fractional powers and roots are obtained in direct readings.

Large industrial corporations use a cylindrical slide rule which gives results correct to five significant figures. 

A HISTORY OF THE SLIDE RULE 

In 1620 Gunter made a straight scale of logarithms and made his calculations with the aid of compasses. 

In 1630 William Oughtred arranged that two of Gunter's scales slide along each other. This was the first introduction of the slide rule.

In 1675 Newton used three logarithmic scales to solve cubic equations and suggested using an indicator. 

In 1859 Lieutenant Mannheim, of the French Army, invented the present form of slide rule that bears his name.

CHAPTER VIII  BY LOGARITHMS 

Suppose you have just returned from a long walk and you are greeted by your friend with the question, "How many inches did you walk today?" You would think the question strange because distances of such length are better measured in miles than in inches. Similarly, if you are asked to measure the length of your schoolroom, you would not think of reporting " of a mile," if the room is 20 feet long. 66 “264 So, too, the method you select in solving a problem should be appropriate to the data given in that problem. If, for example, you are required to solve a right triangle when the given information contains three, four, or more significant figures, you should employ logarithms. On the other hand, if you are working with problems where the given data contain only one or two significant figures, you use natural functions as explained in Chapter II. 

A method of solving right triangles involving logarithms is described below. Another method, the method of "construction," explained in Chapter II is not given here because it is not possible to obtain a sufficiently high degree of accuracy with the ordinary ruler and protractor that the student uses.

CHAPTER IX OBLIQUE TRIANGLES 

In trying to obtain indirect measurements, such as the width of a swamp, the length or width of a dense forest, or the dimensions of inaccessible places in general, it is not always possible or convenient to start with a right triangle. In such cases, we must compute the necessary information from an oblique triangle. For this purpose, we must now establish additional relations for the angles and sides of such triangles. These relations are 

(1) The Law of Sines. 

(2) The Law of Tangents. 

(3) The Law of Cosines. 

(4) The Half-Angle Formulas. 

The Law of Sines. 

Given acute triangle ABC with h, the altitude to side a.

Prove sin A sin B 

sin C 140

sin A sin B sin C 

We, therefore, have the important theorem:

In any triangle, any two sides are proportional to the sines of their opposite angles.

Given obtuse triangle ABC with h, altitude to side a and ZACB obtuse. Prove sin A sin B sin C The proofs for the obtuse as well as for the right triangles are left to the student.

The Law of Cosines. To solve a triangle in which two sides and the included angle are given, we generally use the Law of Tangents. If, however, the given data consist of numbers of only one or two significant figures, it may not be necessary to employ logarithms. In that case, we may use an alternative method based on the Law of Cosines, which we now derive.

Recapitulation of the solution of triangles. For convenience, we may group the solution of oblique triangles into four types. 

It will then be easier to associate a particular method or law with each type.

PLANE TRIGONOMETRY 

(1) Given two angles and one side. Use the Law of Sines. (2) Given three sides. Use the half-angle formulas. For simple data use the Law of Cosines. (3) Given two sides and the included angle. Use the Law of 

Tangents. For simple data use the Law of Cosines. (4) Given two sides and an angle opposite one of these sides. 

Use the Law of Sines. This case is often referred to as the "Ambiguous Case." Let us investigate it now. 

The "Ambiguous Case." When we solve a triangle in which we are given the three sides, or two sides and the included angle, or two angles and the included side, we always arrive at a unique solution. This means that there is only one value for each of the remaining parts of the triangle. If, however, we attempt to solve a triangle when the given data consist of two sides and an angle opposite one of these sides, we sometimes obtain two values for each of the remaining parts. 

A careful examination of the following figures will make it easier to understand this so-called "Ambiguous Case." In each figure, we are given side a, side b, and ZA, where, we must bear in mind, the given angle, ▲ A, is opposite one of the given sides, in this case, a. 

Case I. There will be single values for B, C, and side c in each of the following cases: BA ВА

(1) If A 90° and a > b.

(2) If ≤ A > 90° and a > b.

(3) If ≤ A < 90° and a > b.

Example 1. If we are given a, b, and A of triangle ABC with b less than a, then B may have (a) two solutions, (b) one solution, or (c) no solution. Which is correct (a), (b), or (c)?

In every case where one of the given sides opposite the given angle is greater than the other given side there is only one solution. Therefore, (b) is the correct answer.

CHAPTER X TRIGONOMETRIC EQUATIONS 

Angles may be found by solving given equations. If an equation involves one or more trigonometric functions of the unknown angle, it is known as a trigonometric equation. To solve trigonometric equations we make use of the axioms and the processes learned in solving algebraic equations. What are some of these processes? We also use the formulas learned in trigonometry. 

Example 1. Solve the equation sin A sin 2 A for all values of the unknown angle less than 360°.

Summary. To solve trigonometric equations: 

(1) Reduce functions of the sum or difference of two angles to functions of one of the angles. (2) Reduce different functions to the same function or to sines and cosines. (3) Apply the method of factoring, wherever possible. (4) Check the results.

CHAPTER XI IDENTITIES 

Consider the equality (x + 2)² = x²+4x+4, or the equality sin x + cos² x = 1. These equalities are true for all values of the variable, and are meaningful. Such an equality is called an identity.

Now consider the equality (x + 2)² = 1, or the equality sin x cos x = 1. These equalities are true for certain values of x only. Such an equality is usually referred to as an equation or an equation of condition. 

In this section we discuss identities. Equations of condition were considered in Chapter X. 

The truth of an identity may be established in one of three ways: (1) Transform either side of the identity into the exact form of the other. (2) Reduce both sides of the identity independently to the same form. (3) Reduce both sides of the identity simultaneously to the same form, but be sure that the steps are reversible. 

Examples illustrating the first method. 

Example 1. Prove that sin x +cot x = csc x. 1 + cos x Proof sin x 1 + cos x sin x + cot x COS X 1 + cos x sin x 182

cos x + sin x Examples illustrating the third method. This method resembles the second method in that we reduce each side of the identity to the same form. It differs from the second method in that we operate on both sides of the identity simultaneously. 

Make certain that the steps may be reversed, proceeding from the last statement to the first. The method is similar to what is known as the analytic method used in geometric proofs. 

We say, "This will be true if so and so is true; so and so will be true if some other relation is true." This continues until we reach a step in which the two sides of the identity are the same. We then reverse all the steps.

CHAPTER XII INVERSE TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS 

In the chapter on Trigonometric Equations it was pointed out that for any given angle, there is one and only one value of each of its functions, but that the converse of this statement is not true. For example, tan 45° 1, but there is an infinite number of angles whose tangent is 1, such as 45°, 225°, (360 + 45)°, (360+225)°, or in general, all angles of the form (45 + 360 n)° or (225 + 360 n)°. In both cases we are dealing with functions. 

The tangent of an angle is a function of the angle because its value depends upon the size of the angle. The angle is a function of its tangent because the value of the angle depends upon its tangent. The latter functions are called inverse trigonometric functions, or anti-functions. 

We may summarize in the following words:

The direct trigonometric functions are single-valued while the inverse trigonometric functions are many-valued. 

You will appreciate more fully the contrast between the two types of functions if you will re-examine the graphs of the trigonometric functions. Let us use the sine graph to illustrate the difference. Select any angle, or abscissa, at random and you will find only one value representing its sine, and that is the ordinate corresponding to it. Now place a straight edge parallel to the x-axis and intersecting the curve. There are many points on the curve whose ordinates are equal. This means that there are many angles, the values of whose sines are equal. Make similar interpretations with the graphs of cos x and tan x. 

How to represent inverse functions. Study the following illustrations and comments. 

Direct Function 

Inverse Function tan A = 1 tan-¹ 1 188

(tan 1)-1 means the tangent of 1 radian with an exponent of minus one; 1. 1 it is equivalent to or tan 1r tan 57° 18' The principal or primary value of an inverse trigonometric function is the smallest angle equal to or greater than zero degrees for which the value of the function is true. CHAPTER XIII COMPLEX NUMBERS 

When we solve quadratic equations in one unknown, we occasionally meet an equation whose roots are part real and part imaginary. You will recall that an imaginary number is the square root or any even root of a negative number. We use i to represent - 1. 

Example. Solve for x, x² + 2x + 3 = 0. 2 a 

By the formula x = b± b2 4 ac 

x = 2 -2 ± 2 i√2 2 .. X1 = 

X2 = 

2+V 1 + i√2 1 – i√2. Numbers like these are called complex numbers. The general form of a complex number is a + bi where a and b are real and i = √− 1. √-1. 

Like real numbers, complex numbers can be added, subtracted, multiplied, and divided. We can even perform involution and evolution upon them. These processes may be performed algebraically or graphically. We first describe the algebraic method.

ALGEBRAIC ADDITION AND SUBTRACTION OF COMPLEX NUMBERS 

(1) First write each number in the a + bi form. (2) Add (or subtract) the real parts. (3) Add (or subtract) the imaginary parts. (4) Combine and simplify the two parts for a new complex number which is the required result. 194

GEOMETRIC REPRESENTATION OF COMPLEX NUMBERS 

The graphing of complex numbers is quite simple. Draw two axes perpendicular to each other. Let the horizontal axis be the axis of real numbers and the vertical axis the axis of imaginary numbers. As in Cartesian coordinates, the point of intersection, O, is the origin. Positive real numbers are laid off on XX' to the right of 0; negative real numbers to the left of O. Positive imaginary numbers are laid off on YY' above 0 ; negative imaginary numbers below 0. 

Axis of real

CHAPTER XIV POLAR COORDINATES 

You have already learned a method of plotting points with the aid of cartesian coordinates. In this chapter you will learn another system known as polar coordinates. Compare the two methods carefully and be able to discuss their relative advantages and disadvantages.

Choose a point O anywhere in the plane. Call it the pole or the origin. Let OX be the polar axis. Let the radius vector 

OP be represented by r and the vectorial angle AOP by 0 (called theta). Then (r, 0) are the polar coordinates of the point P. 

This is written P(r, 0). 

Positive angles are those formed by a counter-clockwise rotation from the polar axis; negative angles are those formed by a clockwise rotation. Can we have a negative radius vector? Yes, by laying off the length of r on the terminal side produced through the pole. For example, (- 5, 30°) = (5, 210°).

Example 1. Plot the point (5, 30°). 

Place the protractor on the polar axis and obtain an angle of 30° counter-clockwise. Draw the terminal side OP. Lay 

Then P is the point whose polar off 5 units on the line OP. coordinates are (5, 30°). 200

RELATION BETWEEN RECTANGULAR AND POLAR COORDINATES 

Since we often find it necessary to transform the coordinates of a point from one system to another, we must now discover the relation between the rectangular or cartesian coordinates and the polar coordinates.

DE MOIVRE'S THEOREM 

A very useful theorem dealing with complex numbers was discovered by the mathematician De Moivre and is known as 

De Moivre's Theorem. The theorem may be written in the following manner : (cos x + i sin x)m = cos mx + i sin mx and (cos x 

i sin x)m = cos mx i sin mx or [r(cos x i sin x)]m = rm(cos mx ± i sin mx). 

This theorem is usually proved by the inductive method for which you may not be ready at this time. We shall show here that the theorem is true for the exponent two. may test the theorem for higher exponents. 

To show (cos x + i sin x)² = cos 2 x + i sin 2 x. 

By actual multiplication, (cos + sin x)² 

Grouping terms, cos2x+2 i sin x cos x = (cos² x 

The student
